---- REQUERIMIENTOS DE LA PRÁCTICA ----
 
  
Practice assignment: solving linguistic olympiad problems with large language models



Introduction

In this practice, you will work with large language models (LLMs) of the Gemma family using a free remote API. This approach allows you to focus on advanced prompting and reasoning strategies without the need to download massive model weights or manage infrastructures like Google Colab. By using the Gemma API, you will have access to powerful inference capabilities to tackle one challenging tasks for LLMs: the linguistic olympiad.The linguistic olympiads are international competitions where participants solve puzzles based on data sets from unfamiliar languages. These problems may be described as linguistic sudokus because they do not require prior knowledge of the language in question. Instead, they provide a set of carefully selected example sentences in an unfamiliar target language alongside their translations in a known language. The challenge for the participants is to apply metalinguistic reasoning to decipher the rules of the new language and translate new, unseen phrases.

Why study this in an AI course?

The primary motivation for this assignment is the linguistic gap in modern AI. While there are over 7000 languages spoken worldwide, current language models are only truly fluent in a few dozen, perhaps reaching a hundred at most. This creates a massive digital divide. Linguistic olympiad problems provide a controlled, low-resource scenario. They are intentionally designed to test whether an agent (human or artificial) can extract complex grammatical rules from a very small number of examples. If an AI cannot demonstrate the metalinguistic knowledge required to solve these simplified puzzles, it is unlikely to succeed in real-world low-resource translation or cross-lingual understanding.

The linguistic toolbox: 10 universal concepts

In order to solve these puzzles, participants (usually, pre-university students) must look for patterns that are common across human languages. Here is an explanation of some of the concepts or strategies a participant should consider when analyzing the data:

Agents and patients: identify who is performing the action (the agent) and who is being affected by it (the patient). These roles are the foundation of sentence meaning.
Grammatical functions and cases: languages often mark the role of a noun (subject, direct object, indirect object) using specific endings or changes in the word, known as grammatical cases.
Affixation: many languages build words by adding pieces to a root. Look for prefixes (at the beginning), suffixes (at the end), infixes (inserted inside the root), or circumfixes (wrapped around the root).
Word order: observe the sequence of elements. While English is typically SVO (subject-verb-object), other languages might prefer SOV, VSO, or even free word order.
Morphological strategy: determine if the language is agglutinative, where each piece of information has a clear boundary (like beads on a string), or fusional, where several meanings are packed into a single, inseparable marker.
Noun classes and gender: many languages categorize nouns into groups (human, animal, plant, inanimate, etc.). These categories often dictate how other words in the sentence behave.
Number systems: look beyond the simple singular and plural. Some languages have dual forms for exactly two things, or paucal forms for a small group.
Tense, aspect, and mood: these markers indicate when an action happens (tense), whether it is ongoing or completed (aspect), and the speaker's degree of certainty or intent (mood).
Pro-drop: in some languages, pronouns are omitted if the subject is already clear from the verb ending or the context.
Agreement: notice if adjectives, verbs, or articles change their form to match the gender, number, or class of the noun they accompany.
Task description

Your objective is to evaluate how Gemma models handle these tasks using different reasoning strategies.

1. Initial immersion and dataset acquisition

First, attempt to solve the following linguistic problem involving the Lakota language (spoken by the Sioux people in North America). It is not mandatory to solve the entire problem, but you should spend a few minutes on it, since understanding how you personally extract patterns will help you design better prompts and strategies for the AI.




Second, read how problem 7.1 is solved on pages 260-267 of the book: "Linguistics Olympiad: Training Guide" by Vlad A. Neacșu. Again, it is not mandatory that you fully understand it, but it may inspire you when designing your prompts.

Finally, download the modeLing dataset.This is a benchmark containing this kind of linguistic puzzles specifically designed for LLM reasoning from low-resource languages.

2. Baseline evaluation (zero-shot)

Select a set of problems from the modeLing dataset and perform a zero-shot evaluation. Use the Gemma API to prompt the model with the examples and the test sentences. Use the sacrebleu library to calculate BLEU and chrF scores.

Critical analysis: if the model performs exceptionally well on a rare language, probably it is due to data contamination. Identify languages where the model performs poorly to focus your experimental phase. You may also avoid discarding those languages with excellent zero-shot performance by encrypting their sentences. 

3. Creative strategies for reasoning

The core of this assignment is to propose and implement creative strategies to improve the performance of Gemma models on one or more problems from the modeLing dataset. You are encouraged to be as creative as possible; original and effective approaches will receive higher grades. As a starting point, you may consider the following approximations:

Generator-critic loop: use one instance to propose rules and translations, and another to find inconsistencies.
Recursive bootstrapping: solve the simplest phrases first to build a working grammar before moving to complex ones.
Explicit rule extraction: command the model to generate a formal dictionary and grammar rules before performing the translation task.
Chain of thought with linguistic constraints: guide the model's reasoning using the 10 concepts from the linguistic toolbox above.
Technical implementation

The following Python snippet shows how to interact with the Gemma API. The Gemma API is the same as the non-free Gemini API, but some of the options available for the Gemini models may not work with the Gemma models. Ensure you implement retries to handle potential network issues. You can use the dashboard at Google AI Studio to check the limits and your current usage. Note that there is a limit of 30 requests per minute; therefore, make sure that you pause your scripts at least 2 seconds between one request and the next one. There are also limits for the requests per day and tokens per minute.

from google import genai
import time
import random

API_KEY = "AIzaxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxxx"
MODEL_ID = "gemma-3-27b-it"
NUM_ROUNDS = 20

client = genai.Client(api_key=API_KEY)

centuries = ["XII", "XVII", "XIX", "XXI", "XXV"]
countries = ["Italy", "Vietnam", "Argentina", "Morocco", "Canada"]

for i in range(1, NUM_ROUNDS + 1):
    century = random.choice(centuries)
    country = random.choice(countries)
    
    prompt = f"Write a very short but funny story of 4 or 5 sentences set in the {century} century in {country}."
    
    try:
        response = client.models.generate_content(
            model=MODEL_ID,
            contents=prompt,
            config=genai.types.GenerateContentConfig(
                temperature=1.0,
                top_p=0.95
            ),
        )
        
        print(f"--- story number {i} ---")
        print(f"context: {century} century in {country}")
        print(f"story: {response.text.strip()}")
        print(f"tokens: {response.usage_metadata.total_token_count}")

        print("-" * 50 + "\n")
        
    except Exception as e:
        print(f"error in request {i}: {e}")
    
    if i < NUM_ROUNDS:
        time.sleep(5)


Deliverables

Submit a single PDF containing a technical report (maximum 2000 words excluding potential tables, figures or the text of the prompts that have been used to instruct the models) detailing:

The initial performance analysis and identification of a failed language for the model.
A description of the creative strategies implemented and the prompts used.
Quantitative results (BLEU/chrF) and optional analysis of the errors the model still makes.
Final reflections on the model's ability to perform metalinguistic reasoning.
While extremely advanced AI models can probably do the whole assignment, we discourage their use as lazy unguided prompts often result in detectable hallucinations and a lack of depth that will be penalized. Providing the precise context required for an AI to avoid these errors is frequently more laborious than completing the assignment yourself, which remains the only way to ensure genuine learning and a successful evaluation.



 


---- LO QUE LLEVAMOS ESCRITO ACTUALMENTE DE LA MEMORIA ----
 
  
\chapter{Memory}

\section{Initial performance analysis }

Performance across the selected modeling subset was modest, with an overall BLEU of 16.87, an overall chrF of 36.37, and an exact-match accuracy of only 1.75\% over 8 problems and 57 answers. The distribution of results indicates that the model handled some translation-like \emph{ORDER} tasks comparatively better than the more compositionally demanding non-\emph{ORDER} cases: Engenni 5 was the strongest problem (BLEU 56.85, chrF 64.77), whereas Kalam was the weakest by both evaluation metrics (BLEU 2.23, chrF 13.09). This contrast is also visible at the task-family level, since \emph{ORDER} problems reached mean scores of BLEU 26.84 and chrF 46.14, clearly above the non-\emph{ORDER} averages of BLEU 7.31 and chrF 22.62. Therefore, the initial performance analysis suggests that Gemma can recover some local regularities in relatively structured settings, but still struggles to generalize robustly when the task requires deeper compositional reasoning; under this criterion, Kalam constitutes the clearest failed language/problem for the model at the baseline stage, while Bangime 5, Chimalapa Zoque, and Ainu remain the weakest cases within the \emph{ORDER} backbone.

\section{Creative strategies implemented and the prompts used }


The results of the zero-shot baseline indicate that Gemma's weaknesses are not uniformly distributed across the selected subset, but are concentrated in the most compositionally demanding problems. In absolute terms, the clearest failed language/problem is Kalam, which obtained the lowest baseline scores in the benchmark (BLEU 2.23; chrF 13.09), thereby providing the strongest evidence of failure under the initial evaluation. However, because the core experimental backbone of this study is the \emph{ORDER} family, the most relevant hard failure case for the subsequent analysis is Chimalapa Zoque: although it is not the weakest problem in the subset as a whole, it combines high difficulty (5) with clearly weak baseline performance (BLEU 14.46; chrF 37.64; exact match 0.00) and the highest structural richness among the hard \emph{ORDER} candidates (18 support examples and 12 questions). Thus, the initial performance analysis supports a two-level conclusion: Kalam is the strongest global failure case, whereas Chimalapa Zoque is the most methodologically appropriate failed \emph{ORDER} language for controlled follow-up experimentation.

\section{Quantitative results }

Quantitatively, the proposed prompting strategies did not improve performance over the direct zero-shot baseline. On the main target problem, Chimalapa Zoque, the original baseline remained clearly stronger, with BLEU 14.46 and chrF 37.64, whereas all three multi-pass strategies produced lower scores: recursive bootstrapping, which was the strongest among the modified prompts, decreased to BLEU 8.70 and chrF 15.87; explicit rule extraction fell to BLEU 4.03 and chrF 11.74; and the generator-critic pipeline deteriorated further to BLEU 0.48 and chrF 7.01. This negative trend also persisted at the selected-subset level under the controlled counterfactual comparison in which only the target problem was replaced: even the best alternative strategy reduced the overall benchmark from BLEU 16.87 and chrF 36.37 to BLEU 15.19 and chrF 30.49. Therefore, within this experimental setup, adding more structured inference-time reasoning did not yield better metalinguistic performance; instead, the simplest direct zero-shot configuration remained the most effective quantitative setting.





----- FICHEROS CON INFORMACIÓN RELEVANTE PARA DESARROLLAR EL APARTADO EN LA MEMORIA -----
  
   
-- section_16_qualitative_error_analysis_report.txt --

Qualitative error analysis report

For the main target problem, Chimalapa Zoque, the direct zero-shot baseline already performs weakly (BLEU 14.46, chrF 37.64, Exact Match 0.00), and every multi-pass prompting strategy degrades that baseline. The least harmful strategy is recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, but even that configuration falls to BLEU 8.70 and chrF 15.87. The goal of this section is therefore diagnostic: to identify which error categories actually dominate the failed improved runs, and to ensure that the narrative follows the computed evidence.

The denominator-normalized comparison is essential here. The baseline profile is computed over 12 unique target answers, whereas the improved aggregate profile is computed over 36 answer records because it pools the three tested strategies. The baseline target profile is mixed: structural_error 3/12 (0.25 per answer), morphological_error 3/12 (0.25 per answer), lexical_error 4/12 (0.33 per answer). By contrast, the aggregate improved-run profile is dominated by lexical_error 30/36 (0.83 per answer), hallucinated_rule 11/36 (0.31 per answer), structural_error 5/36 (0.14 per answer). This means the improved runs are not mainly characterized by morphology or semantic-role confusion under the current heuristics; instead, they are dominated by lexical failure, hallucinated-rule behaviour, and a smaller amount of structural error.

For the strongest strategy under the current ranking, recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, the answer-level profile is: lexical_error 9/12 (0.75 per answer), hallucinated_rule 1/12 (0.08 per answer), structural_error 2/12 (0.17 per answer), morphological_error 0/12 (0.00 per answer), semantic_role_confusion 0/12 (0.00 per answer). This strategy still improves only 4 of 12 answers while worsening 8, so even the best multi-pass configuration remains clearly below the direct zero-shot baseline.

Structural error is therefore present, but it should be described as secondary rather than dominant. Under recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, structure-related signals appear in 2/12 (0.17 per answer). This supports the claim that some answers recover part of the right material but assemble it in the wrong order or global configuration. The strongest structure-related instances under recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass appear in question(s) 4, 5.

Morphological error should not be presented as a dominant improved-run category. The improved aggregate profile contains 0/36 (0.00 per answer), and recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass contains 0/12 (0.00 per answer). The only place where morphology remains visible is the baseline target profile, where it appears in 3/12 (0.25 per answer). Accordingly, the final report should treat morphology as a baseline-side or weak residual signal here, not as one of the main improved-run dominant classes.

Lexical error is the clearest dominant class in the improved runs. Under recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, lexical signals appear in 9/12 (0.75 per answer), and across all improved strategies they appear in 30/36 (0.83 per answer). This is why the section should not claim that the bottleneck is mainly non-lexical. In the present run, lexical failure is central, especially in question(s) 7, 9, 8.

Semantic-role confusion is not robustly supported as an independent dominant category in this run. The improved aggregate profile contains 0/36 (0.00 per answer), and recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass contains 0/12 (0.00 per answer). Accordingly, the final interpretation should not describe semantic-role confusion as recurring unless a future rerun actually raises these counts.

Hallucinated-rule behaviour is now supported not only by output degradation but also by direct inspection of the intermediate reasoning texts. For recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, the non-final stages contain 3 rule-claim lines, 1 of which have no explicit support-token grounding, and 0 strong-claim lines. This does not prove hallucination in a strict causal sense, but it does provide memo-level evidence that unsupported generalizations are appearing before the final answers are produced.

Incomplete decomposition also remains strongly supported by the answer-level comparison. The best strategy, recursive_bootstrapping_two_pass, improves 4 answers but worsens 8, with mean sentence-level chrF delta -22.78. More elaborate strategies become progressively worse, which is consistent with the idea that extra reasoning passes amplify drift instead of stabilizing the solution.

At a broader level, this pattern also helps explain why Gemma behaves much better on easier ORDER cases than on a structurally richer hard case such as Chimalapa Zoque. In the baseline, the easier ORDER controls performed much better, especially Engenni 5 (BLEU 56.85, chrF 64.77) and Rapa Nui 5 (BLEU 32.58, chrF 57.22). The contamination check also remained stable for Engenni 5 (chrF retention 1.2590), Rapa Nui 5 (chrF retention 0.9588), which is more compatible with genuine structure-based reasoning than with simple dependence on original lexical surface forms. This is compatible with the idea that the model can exploit genuine structural regularities when the system is simpler, but struggles once many interacting regularities must be tracked simultaneously.

The safest final interpretation is therefore fully data-driven: in the current run, the dominant improved-run pattern is lexical failure, hallucinated-rule behaviour, and some structural error. Morphological instability and semantic-role confusion should not be presented as dominant improved-run categories here, because the calibrated counts do not support that claim. If a future rerun changes the counts, the narrative should change with them.

----- CELDAS PRINCIPALES DEL CÓDIGO CORRESPONDIENTE DEL PROYECTO JUNTO CON LAS SALIDAS DE EJECUCIÓN NECESARIO PARA DESARROLLAR EL APARTADO EN LA MEMORIA -----
 

#CELL 11.2 - Main target selection helpers, decision logic, summaries, and artifact-saving utilities

def make_json_serializable(value: Any) -> Any:
    if isinstance(value, dict):
        return {
            str(key): make_json_serializable(subvalue)
            for key, subvalue in value.items()
        }

    if isinstance(value, list):
        return [make_json_serializable(item) for item in value]

    if isinstance(value, tuple):
        return [make_json_serializable(item) for item in value]

    if isinstance(value, Path):
        return str(value)

    if value is pd.NA:
        return None

    if hasattr(value, "item"):
        try:
            return value.item()
        except Exception:
            pass

    if isinstance(value, float) and math.isnan(value):
        return None

    return value


def compute_structural_richness_score(problem_result: Dict[str, Any]) -> int:
    return int(problem_result["num_support_examples"]) + int(problem_result["num_questions"])


def identify_global_failure_problem_name_without_section_9_dependency(
    zero_shot_problem_results: List[Dict[str, Any]],
    ranking_metric: str,
    secondary_metric: str,
) -> str:
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(ranking_metric)
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(secondary_metric)

    if not zero_shot_problem_results:
        raise ValueError("Cannot identify the global failure problem from an empty result list.")

    ranked_problem_results = sorted(
        zero_shot_problem_results,
        key=lambda problem_result: (
            float(problem_result[ranking_metric]),
            float(problem_result[secondary_metric]),
            float(problem_result["exact_match_accuracy"]),
            int(problem_result["subset_order"]),
        ),
    )

    return str(ranked_problem_results[0]["problem_name"])


def build_order_vs_non_order_summary_from_problem_selection_dataframe(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
) -> pd.DataFrame:
    if problem_selection_dataframe.empty:
        raise ValueError("Cannot build ORDER vs non-ORDER summary from an empty dataframe.")

    summary_rows: List[Dict[str, Any]] = []

    for evaluation_scope_name, is_target_order_scope in [
        ("ORDER_only", True),
        ("non_ORDER_only", False),
    ]:
        scope_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe[
            problem_selection_dataframe["is_target_task_family"] == is_target_order_scope
        ].copy()

        if scope_dataframe.empty:
            continue

        summary_rows.append(
            {
                "evaluation_scope": evaluation_scope_name,
                "num_problems": int(len(scope_dataframe)),
                "num_answers": int(scope_dataframe["num_questions"].sum()),
                "mean_difficulty": float(scope_dataframe["difficulty"].mean()),
                "mean_bleu_score": float(scope_dataframe["bleu_score"].mean()),
                "mean_chrf_score": float(scope_dataframe["chrf_score"].mean()),
                "mean_exact_match_accuracy": float(
                    scope_dataframe["exact_match_accuracy"].mean()
                ),
            }
        )

    return pd.DataFrame(summary_rows).reset_index(drop=True)


def build_main_target_problem_selection_dataframe(
    zero_shot_problem_results: List[Dict[str, Any]],
    target_task_family: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
    min_primary_difficulty: int = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY,
    primary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    secondary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
) -> pd.DataFrame:
    if not zero_shot_problem_results:
        raise ValueError(
            "Cannot build the main-target selection dataframe from empty zero-shot results."
        )

    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(primary_failure_metric)
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(secondary_failure_metric)

    global_failure_problem_name = identify_global_failure_problem_name_without_section_9_dependency(
        zero_shot_problem_results=zero_shot_problem_results,
        ranking_metric=primary_failure_metric,
        secondary_metric=secondary_failure_metric,
    )

    summary_rows: List[Dict[str, Any]] = []

    for problem_result in sorted(
        zero_shot_problem_results,
        key=lambda item: item["subset_order"],
    ):
        task_family_list = list(problem_result["task_family"])
        difficulty_value = int(problem_result["difficulty"])
        is_target_task_family = target_task_family in task_family_list
        structural_richness_score = compute_structural_richness_score(problem_result)

        summary_rows.append(
            {
                "subset_order": int(problem_result["subset_order"]),
                "problem_name": problem_result["problem_name"],
                "task_family": ", ".join(task_family_list),
                "difficulty": difficulty_value,
                "subset_group": problem_result["subset_group"],
                "selection_role": problem_result["selection_role"],
                "reasoning_focus": problem_result["reasoning_focus"],
                "num_support_examples": int(problem_result["num_support_examples"]),
                "num_questions": int(problem_result["num_questions"]),
                "bleu_score": float(problem_result["bleu_score"]),
                "chrf_score": float(problem_result["chrf_score"]),
                "exact_match_accuracy": float(problem_result["exact_match_accuracy"]),
                "usage_total_token_count": problem_result.get("usage_total_token_count"),
                "is_target_task_family": bool(is_target_task_family),
                "is_global_failure_problem": (
                    problem_result["problem_name"] == global_failure_problem_name
                ),
                "is_hard_target_candidate": bool(
                    is_target_task_family and difficulty_value >= min_primary_difficulty
                ),
                "structural_richness_score": structural_richness_score,
            }
        )

    problem_selection_dataframe = (
        pd.DataFrame(summary_rows)
        .sort_values(by="subset_order")
        .reset_index(drop=True)
    )

    target_family_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe[
        problem_selection_dataframe["is_target_task_family"]
    ].copy()

    if target_family_dataframe.empty:
        raise ValueError(
            f"No problems from task family '{target_task_family}' are available."
        )

    target_family_failure_rank_dataframe = target_family_dataframe.sort_values(
        by=[
            primary_failure_metric,
            secondary_failure_metric,
            "difficulty",
            "subset_order",
        ],
        ascending=[True, True, False, True],
    ).reset_index(drop=True)

    target_family_failure_rank_dataframe["target_family_failure_rank"] = range(
        1,
        len(target_family_failure_rank_dataframe) + 1,
    )

    target_family_richness_rank_dataframe = target_family_dataframe.sort_values(
        by=[
            "difficulty",
            "structural_richness_score",
            "num_support_examples",
            "num_questions",
            "subset_order",
        ],
        ascending=[False, False, False, False, True],
    ).reset_index(drop=True)

    target_family_richness_rank_dataframe["target_family_structural_richness_rank"] = range(
        1,
        len(target_family_richness_rank_dataframe) + 1,
    )

    problem_selection_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe.merge(
        target_family_failure_rank_dataframe[
            ["problem_name", "target_family_failure_rank"]
        ],
        on="problem_name",
        how="left",
    )

    problem_selection_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe.merge(
        target_family_richness_rank_dataframe[
            ["problem_name", "target_family_structural_richness_rank"]
        ],
        on="problem_name",
        how="left",
    )

    return problem_selection_dataframe


def build_ranked_hard_target_candidate_dataframe(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
    target_task_family: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
    min_primary_difficulty: int = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY,
    primary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    secondary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
) -> pd.DataFrame:
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(primary_failure_metric)
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(secondary_failure_metric)

    hard_target_candidate_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe[
        (problem_selection_dataframe["is_target_task_family"])
        & (problem_selection_dataframe["difficulty"] >= min_primary_difficulty)
    ].copy()

    if hard_target_candidate_dataframe.empty:
        raise ValueError(
            "No hard target candidates are available under the configured constraints."
        )

    return (
        hard_target_candidate_dataframe.sort_values(
            by=[
                "difficulty",
                "structural_richness_score",
                primary_failure_metric,
                secondary_failure_metric,
                "subset_order",
            ],
            ascending=[False, False, True, True, True],
        )
        .reset_index(drop=True)
    )

def select_main_and_backup_targets(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
    target_task_family: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
    min_primary_difficulty: int = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY,
    primary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    secondary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    hard_target_candidate_dataframe = build_ranked_hard_target_candidate_dataframe(
        problem_selection_dataframe=problem_selection_dataframe,
        target_task_family=target_task_family,
        min_primary_difficulty=min_primary_difficulty,
        primary_failure_metric=primary_failure_metric,
        secondary_failure_metric=secondary_failure_metric,
    )

    primary_target_row = hard_target_candidate_dataframe.iloc[0]

    remaining_hard_target_candidate_dataframe = hard_target_candidate_dataframe[
        hard_target_candidate_dataframe["problem_name"] != primary_target_row["problem_name"]
    ].copy()

    secondary_backup_row = None
    if not remaining_hard_target_candidate_dataframe.empty:
        secondary_backup_row = (
            remaining_hard_target_candidate_dataframe.sort_values(
                by=[
                    primary_failure_metric,
                    secondary_failure_metric,
                    "structural_richness_score",
                    "subset_order",
                ],
                ascending=[True, True, False, True],
            )
            .reset_index(drop=True)
            .iloc[0]
        )

    excluded_problem_names = {primary_target_row["problem_name"]}
    if secondary_backup_row is not None:
        excluded_problem_names.add(secondary_backup_row["problem_name"])

    remaining_order_problem_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe[
        (problem_selection_dataframe["is_target_task_family"])
        & (~problem_selection_dataframe["problem_name"].isin(excluded_problem_names))
    ].copy()

    intermediate_bridge_row = None
    if not remaining_order_problem_dataframe.empty:
        intermediate_bridge_row = (
            remaining_order_problem_dataframe.sort_values(
                by=[
                    primary_failure_metric,
                    secondary_failure_metric,
                    "difficulty",
                    "subset_order",
                ],
                ascending=[True, True, False, True],
            )
            .reset_index(drop=True)
            .iloc[0]
        )

    selection_decision = {
        "target_task_family": target_task_family,
        "min_primary_difficulty": int(min_primary_difficulty),
        "primary_failure_metric": primary_failure_metric,
        "secondary_failure_metric": secondary_failure_metric,
        "primary_target_priority_order": [
            "difficulty (descending)",
            "structural_richness_score (descending)",
            f"{primary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            f"{secondary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            "subset_order (ascending)",
        ],
        "secondary_backup_priority_order": [
            f"{primary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            f"{secondary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            "structural_richness_score (descending)",
            "subset_order (ascending)",
        ],
        "intermediate_bridge_priority_order": [
            f"{primary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            f"{secondary_failure_metric} (ascending)",
            "difficulty (descending)",
            "subset_order (ascending)",
        ],
        "primary_target_name": str(primary_target_row["problem_name"]),
        "primary_target_row": make_json_serializable(primary_target_row.to_dict()),
        "secondary_backup_name": (
            None if secondary_backup_row is None else str(secondary_backup_row["problem_name"])
        ),
        "secondary_backup_row": (
            None if secondary_backup_row is None
            else make_json_serializable(secondary_backup_row.to_dict())
        ),
        "intermediate_bridge_name": (
            None if intermediate_bridge_row is None else str(intermediate_bridge_row["problem_name"])
        ),
        "intermediate_bridge_row": (
            None if intermediate_bridge_row is None
            else make_json_serializable(intermediate_bridge_row.to_dict())
        ),
        "ranked_hard_target_candidates": dataframe_to_json_serializable_records(
            hard_target_candidate_dataframe
        ),
    }

    return selection_decision

def build_main_target_selection_report_text(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
    selection_decision: Dict[str, Any],
    contamination_comparison_dataframe: Optional[pd.DataFrame] = None,
    primary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    secondary_failure_metric: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    target_task_family: str = MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
) -> str:
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(primary_failure_metric)
    validate_initial_performance_metric_name(secondary_failure_metric)

    if problem_selection_dataframe.empty:
        raise ValueError("problem_selection_dataframe cannot be empty.")

    global_failure_row = (
        problem_selection_dataframe.sort_values(
            by=[
                primary_failure_metric,
                secondary_failure_metric,
                "exact_match_accuracy",
                "subset_order",
            ],
            ascending=[True, True, True, True],
        )
        .reset_index(drop=True)
        .iloc[0]
    )

    order_problem_dataframe = problem_selection_dataframe[
        problem_selection_dataframe["is_target_task_family"]
    ].copy()

    if order_problem_dataframe.empty:
        raise ValueError(
            f"No problems from task family '{target_task_family}' are available."
        )

    order_problem_failure_rank_dataframe = order_problem_dataframe.sort_values(
        by=[
            primary_failure_metric,
            secondary_failure_metric,
            "difficulty",
            "subset_order",
        ],
        ascending=[True, True, False, True],
    ).reset_index(drop=True)

    primary_target_row = selection_decision.get("primary_target_row")
    secondary_backup_row = selection_decision.get("secondary_backup_row")
    intermediate_bridge_row = selection_decision.get("intermediate_bridge_row")

    if primary_target_row is None:
        raise ValueError("selection_decision does not contain a primary target row.")

    order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe = (
        build_order_vs_non_order_summary_from_problem_selection_dataframe(
            problem_selection_dataframe
        )
    )

    order_scope_row = None
    non_order_scope_row = None

    if not order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe.empty:
        order_scope_matches = order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe[
            order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe["evaluation_scope"] == "ORDER_only"
        ]
        non_order_scope_matches = order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe[
            order_vs_non_order_summary_dataframe["evaluation_scope"] == "non_ORDER_only"
        ]

        if not order_scope_matches.empty:
            order_scope_row = order_scope_matches.iloc[0]

        if not non_order_scope_matches.empty:
            non_order_scope_row = non_order_scope_matches.iloc[0]

    weakest_order_problem_text = ", ".join(
        [
            (
                f"{row['problem_name']} "
                f"(difficulty {int(row['difficulty'])}, BLEU {row['bleu_score']:.2f}, "
                f"chrF {row['chrf_score']:.2f}, Exact Match {row['exact_match_accuracy']:.2f})"
            )
            for _, row in order_problem_failure_rank_dataframe.head(3).iterrows()
        ]
    )

    paragraph_1 = (
        f"The weakest problem in the evaluated selected subset is "
        f"{global_failure_row['problem_name']} (BLEU {global_failure_row['bleu_score']:.2f}, "
        f"chrF {global_failure_row['chrf_score']:.2f}, Exact Match "
        f"{global_failure_row['exact_match_accuracy']:.2f}). However, this problem belongs to "
        f"the {global_failure_row['task_family']} family rather than to the translation-like "
        f"{target_task_family} backbone that structures the evaluated subset. For that reason, "
        f"the main target for the improvement phase should not be chosen by absolute subset-wide "
        f"weakness alone, but by combining failure evidence with methodological coherence."
    )

    if order_scope_row is not None and non_order_scope_row is not None:
        paragraph_2 = (
            f"Within the {target_task_family} subset, the weakest or most demanding candidates are "
            f"{weakest_order_problem_text}. The ORDER family also performs substantially better "
            f"than the non-ORDER set in the baseline (mean BLEU "
            f"{order_scope_row['mean_bleu_score']:.2f} vs {non_order_scope_row['mean_bleu_score']:.2f}; "
            f"mean chrF {order_scope_row['mean_chrf_score']:.2f} vs "
            f"{non_order_scope_row['mean_chrf_score']:.2f}), which reinforces the idea that the "
            f"later improvement phase should remain centered on ORDER rather than switching to a "
            f"different task family."
        )
    else:
        paragraph_2 = (
            f"Within the {target_task_family} subset, the weakest or most demanding candidates are "
            f"{weakest_order_problem_text}. This keeps the improvement phase centered on the same "
            f"translation-like task family used as the main experimental backbone."
        )

    paragraph_3 = (
        f"The primary-selection rule used here is a combined priority, not a pure failure ranking. "
        f"It filters the benchmark to {target_task_family} problems with difficulty at least "
        f"{MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY}, and then ranks them by difficulty, "
        f"structural richness, {primary_failure_metric}, {secondary_failure_metric}, and "
        f"subset order. Under that rule, the most defensible primary target is "
        f"{primary_target_row['problem_name']}. It is a {target_task_family} problem of difficulty "
        f"{int(primary_target_row['difficulty'])}, it shows weak zero-shot performance "
        f"(BLEU {primary_target_row['bleu_score']:.2f}, chrF {primary_target_row['chrf_score']:.2f}, "
        f"Exact Match {primary_target_row['exact_match_accuracy']:.2f}), and it is the structurally "
        f"richest hard ORDER case in the subset, with "
        f"{int(primary_target_row['num_support_examples'])} support examples and "
        f"{int(primary_target_row['num_questions'])} questions "
        f"(structural richness score {int(primary_target_row['structural_richness_score'])}). "
        f"These properties make it especially suitable for strategy-based improvements such as "
        f"explicit rule extraction, recursive bootstrapping, generator-critic verification, and "
        f"linguistically constrained chain-of-thought prompting."
    )

    paragraph_4 = ""
    if secondary_backup_row is not None:
        paragraph_4 = (
            f"The secondary backup target is {secondary_backup_row['problem_name']}. After removing "
            f"the primary target, it is the best remaining hard ORDER candidate under the backup "
            f"ranking rule, which orders the remaining candidates by {primary_failure_metric}, "
            f"{secondary_failure_metric}, structural richness, and subset order. It also belongs to "
            f"the {target_task_family} family, has difficulty "
            f"{int(secondary_backup_row['difficulty'])}, and remains clearly weak in the baseline "
            f"(BLEU {secondary_backup_row['bleu_score']:.2f}, chrF "
            f"{secondary_backup_row['chrf_score']:.2f}, Exact Match "
            f"{secondary_backup_row['exact_match_accuracy']:.2f}). This makes it a genuine hard "
            f"fallback for qualitative error analysis or for a secondary comparison if the primary "
            f"target yields unstable results."
        )
    else:
        paragraph_4 = (
            f"No secondary backup target is available under the current constraints, because only one "
            f"hard {target_task_family} candidate satisfies the configured selection rule."
        )

    paragraph_5 = ""
    if intermediate_bridge_row is not None:
        paragraph_5 = (
            f"By contrast, {intermediate_bridge_row['problem_name']} is better treated as an "
            f"intermediate bridge problem than as the flagship improvement target. It has chrF "
            f"{intermediate_bridge_row['chrf_score']:.2f}, compared with "
            f"{primary_target_row['chrf_score']:.2f} for {primary_target_row['problem_name']}, "
            f"but its difficulty is only {int(intermediate_bridge_row['difficulty'])}. That makes it "
            f"useful as a weaker intermediate ORDER case rather than as the main target for the most "
            f"ambitious prompting strategies."
        )

    contamination_paragraph = ""
    if contamination_comparison_dataframe is not None and not contamination_comparison_dataframe.empty:
        stable_rows = contamination_comparison_dataframe[
            contamination_comparison_dataframe["interpretation"]
            == "stable_consistent_with_structure_based_reasoning"
        ].copy()

        if len(stable_rows) == len(contamination_comparison_dataframe):
            stable_problem_names = ", ".join(stable_rows["source_problem_name"].tolist())
            contamination_paragraph = (
                f"The contamination-validity evidence is also compatible with preserving an "
                f"ORDER-centered experimental path: the strongest ORDER controls tested under "
                f"surface obfuscation ({stable_problem_names}) remained stable or even improved, "
                f"which suggests that the ORDER backbone is not being driven merely by superficial "
                f"lexical memorization."
            )

    if secondary_backup_row is not None:
        paragraph_6 = (
            f"Therefore, the final decision for this section is to define "
            f"{primary_target_row['problem_name']} as the main experimental target and "
            f"{secondary_backup_row['problem_name']} as the secondary backup. This is a stronger "
            f"methodological choice than simply selecting the absolute worst problem in the "
            f"evaluated selected subset, because it preserves coherence with the ORDER-centered "
            f"evaluation design while still focusing on a problem that is clearly difficult, "
            f"structurally rich, and well suited for testing whether more advanced prompting "
            f"strategies can produce measurable improvements over the direct zero-shot baseline."
        )
    else:
        paragraph_6 = (
            f"Therefore, the final decision for this section is to define "
            f"{primary_target_row['problem_name']} as the main experimental target. This is a "
            f"stronger methodological choice than simply selecting the absolute worst problem in "
            f"the evaluated selected subset, because it preserves coherence with the ORDER-centered "
            f"evaluation design while still focusing on a problem that is clearly difficult, "
            f"structurally rich, and well suited for testing whether more advanced prompting "
            f"strategies can produce measurable improvements over the direct zero-shot baseline."
        )

    report_paragraphs = [
        "Selection of the main target problem for improvement",
        "",
        paragraph_1,
        paragraph_2,
        paragraph_3,
        paragraph_4,
    ]

    if paragraph_5:
        report_paragraphs.append(paragraph_5)

    if contamination_paragraph:
        report_paragraphs.append(contamination_paragraph)

    report_paragraphs.append(paragraph_6)

    return "\n\n".join(report_paragraphs)


def build_main_target_selection_payload(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
    selection_decision: Dict[str, Any],
    contamination_comparison_dataframe: Optional[pd.DataFrame] = None,
) -> Dict[str, Any]:
    payload: Dict[str, Any] = {
        "analysis_name": "main_target_problem_selection",
        "target_task_family": MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
        "primary_failure_metric": MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
        "secondary_failure_metric": MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
        "min_primary_difficulty": MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY,
        "structural_richness_formula_name": MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_STRUCTURAL_RICHNESS_FORMULA_NAME,
        "problem_selection_records": dataframe_to_json_serializable_records(
            problem_selection_dataframe
        ),
        "selection_decision": make_json_serializable(selection_decision),
    }

    if contamination_comparison_dataframe is not None and not contamination_comparison_dataframe.empty:
        payload["contamination_comparison_records"] = dataframe_to_json_serializable_records(
            contamination_comparison_dataframe
        )

    return payload


def save_main_target_selection_artifacts(
    problem_selection_dataframe: pd.DataFrame,
    selection_decision: Dict[str, Any],
    output_directory: Path,
    contamination_comparison_dataframe: Optional[pd.DataFrame] = None,
) -> Dict[str, Path]:
    output_directory.mkdir(parents=True, exist_ok=True)

    hard_target_candidate_dataframe = build_ranked_hard_target_candidate_dataframe(
        problem_selection_dataframe=problem_selection_dataframe,
        target_task_family=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
        min_primary_difficulty=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_MIN_PRIMARY_DIFFICULTY,
        primary_failure_metric=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
        secondary_failure_metric=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
    )

    report_text = build_main_target_selection_report_text(
        problem_selection_dataframe=problem_selection_dataframe,
        selection_decision=selection_decision,
        contamination_comparison_dataframe=contamination_comparison_dataframe,
        primary_failure_metric=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_PRIMARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
        secondary_failure_metric=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_SECONDARY_FAILURE_METRIC,
        target_task_family=MAIN_TARGET_SELECTION_TARGET_TASK_FAMILY,
    )

    payload = build_main_target_selection_payload(
        problem_selection_dataframe=problem_selection_dataframe,
        selection_decision=selection_decision,
        contamination_comparison_dataframe=contamination_comparison_dataframe,
    )

    main_target_selection_json_output_path = (
        output_directory / "main_target_problem_selection_records.json"
    )
    main_target_selection_summary_csv_output_path = (
        output_directory / "main_target_problem_selection_summary.csv"
    )
    main_target_selection_hard_candidates_csv_output_path = (
        output_directory / "main_target_problem_selection_hard_candidates.csv"
    )
    main_target_selection_report_output_path = (
        output_directory / "main_target_problem_selection_report.txt"
    )

    save_json_file(payload, main_target_selection_json_output_path)

    problem_selection_dataframe.to_csv(
        main_target_selection_summary_csv_output_path,
        index=False,
        encoding="utf-8",
    )

    hard_target_candidate_dataframe.to_csv(
        main_target_selection_hard_candidates_csv_output_path,
        index=False,
        encoding="utf-8",
    )

    with open(main_target_selection_report_output_path, "w", encoding="utf-8") as report_file:
        report_file.write(report_text)

    return {
        "main_target_selection_json_output_path": main_target_selection_json_output_path,
        "main_target_selection_summary_csv_output_path": main_target_selection_summary_csv_output_path,
        "main_target_selection_hard_candidates_csv_output_path": (
            main_target_selection_hard_candidates_csv_output_path
        ),
        "main_target_selection_report_output_path": main_target_selection_report_output_path,
    }




#CELL 11.3 - Run the main target problem selection and save the corresponding artifacts

main_target_selection_problem_summary_dataframe = pd.DataFrame()
main_target_selection_decision: Dict[str, Any] = {}
main_target_selection_saved_paths: Dict[str, Path] = {}
main_target_selection_report_text = ""

optional_contamination_comparison_dataframe: Optional[pd.DataFrame] = None
if (

):

Preview of the Section 11 report:

Selection of the main target problem for improvement



The weakest problem in the evaluated selected subset is Kalam (BLEU 2.23, chrF 13.09, Exact Match 0.00). However, this problem belongs to the SEM family rather than to the translation-like ORDER backbone that structures the evaluated subset. For that reason, the main target for the improvement phase should not be chosen by absolute subset-wide weakness alone, but by combining failure evidence with methodological coherence.

Within the ORDER subset, the weakest or most demanding candidates are Bangime 5 (difficulty 3, BLEU 13.44, chrF 30.82, Exact Match 0.00), Chimalapa Zoque (difficulty 5, BLEU 14.46, chrF 37.64, Exact Match 0.00), Ainu (difficulty 5, BLEU 16.85, chrF 40.26, Exact Match 0.00). The ORDER family also performs substantially better than the non-ORDER set in the baseline (mean BLEU 26.84 vs 7.31; mean chrF 46.14 vs 22.62), which reinforces the idea that the later improvement phase should remain centered on ORDER rather than switching to a different task family.

The primary-selection rule used here is a combined priority, not a pure failure ranking. It filters the benchmark to ORDER problems with difficulty at least 5, and then ranks them by difficulty, structural richness, chrf_score, bleu_score, and subset order. Under that rule, the most defensible primary target is Chimalapa Zoque. It is a ORDER problem of difficulty 5, it shows weak zero-shot performance (BLEU 14.46, chrF 37.64, Exact Match 0.00), and it is the structurally richest hard ORDER case in the subset, with 18 support examples and 12 questions (structural richness score 30). These properties make it especially suitable for strategy-based improvements such as explicit rule extraction, recursive bootstrapping, generator-critic verification, and linguistically constrained chain-of-thought prompting.

The secondary backup target is Ainu. After removing the primary target, it is the best remaining hard ORDER candidate under the backup ranking rule, which orders the remaining candidates by chrf_score, bleu_score, structural richness, and subset order. It also belongs to the ORDER family, has difficulty 5, and remains clearly weak in the baseline (BLEU 16.85, chrF 40.26, Exact Match 0.00). This makes it a genuine hard fallback for qualitative error analysis or for a secondary comparison if the primary target yields unstable results.

By contrast, Bangime 5 is better treated as an intermediate bridge problem than as the flagship improvement target. It has chrF 30.82, compared with 37.64 for Chimalapa Zoque, but its difficulty is only 3. That makes it useful as a weaker intermediate ORDER case rather than as the main target for the most ambitious prompting strategies.

The contamination-validity evidence is also compatible with preserving an ORDER-centered experimental path: the strongest ORDER controls tested under surface obfuscation (Engenni 5, Rapa Nui 5) remained stable or even improved, which suggests that the ORDER backbone is not being driven merely by superficial lexical memorization.

Therefore, the final decision for this section is to define Chimalapa Zoque as the main experimental target and Ainu as the secondary backup. This is a stronger methodological choice than simply selecting the absolute worst problem in the evaluated selected subset, because it preserves coherence with the ORDER-centered evaluation design while still focusing on a problem that is clearly difficult, structurally rich, and well suited for testing whether more advanced prompting strategies can produce measurable improvements over the direct zero-shot baseline.
